## 3. Which Cooking Spray is Best? | America's Test Kitchen
**Channel:** America's Test Kitchen | **Views:** 75K | **Date:** 4 days ago | **Duration:** 3:14 | **ID:** bGYEcwKjbAk
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=bGYEcwKjbAk

### Transcript:
- Sometimes in life, the simplest things
get overly complicated. Cooking spray is a good example. Now, if you're out shopping
in the supermarket, you've probably seen there are lots of choices when it comes to oil. It's no longer just vegetable oil. You can have olive oil,
you can have avocado oil, you can have grape seed oil,
you can even have coconut oil. Well, I've got news for you, you're using so little cooking spray, you can't taste the difference, you won't see the difference
between any of these. It's absolutely meaningless. You can use whatever oil you want. What is important is
the delivery mechanism. Now, these four are all
traditional cooking sprays. By traditional, I mean they
have a propellant inside them. There's either propane or butane inside the
can along with the oil. That propellant is liquid, but when you hit the
nozzle, it turns to a gas and it propels the oil out
and you get a nice even spray. Most of these also have
lecithin, an emulsifier, so you get really fine coverage. It helps it get into every
nook and cranny on a Bundt pan. The lecithin also makes cleanup easier. The one thing we don't like
about these is the lecithin does reduce the smoke point. So if you're doing high heat
sautéing with cooking spray, these traditional models
are not nearly as effective. So therefore, there is this
new style of propellantless. These all have actually a
bag of oil inside the can, and there's either nitrogen or compressed air that's
sort of surrounding that bag. And when you hit the
nozzle, it squeezes the bag and basically splurts the oil out. Right? Splurting not propelling. Doesn't sound like a good thing. And in fact, with the exception of this one here from Chosen
Foods, we don't recommend any of the propellantless cooking sprays. Because honestly,
they're difficult to use. But let me show you
what the difference is. So I've got our favorite, from Pam, traditional cooking spray. You notice that the nozzle
is pointing straight ahead, so that's gonna make
it very intuitive as to where I should be pointing the can. I'm going to demo here, and you see here I've
got a pretty nice circle. And if I were trying to get the oil, let's say into a small ramekin, it would really go where I want it to go. So I'm gonna take the Chosen Foods. Now, this is the only one we liked of the kind without the propellant. It's got this nozzle going up. So the first time you
use it, I promise you, you're gonna spray the counter. I'm gonna try not to do that
today. All right, let's see. Well, I actually did kind of
spray the counter a little bit. You see, it comes out much less even. So if you wanna keep it simple and you don't really care
about high heat sautéing, which is really the only time you'd want to be using a propellantless,
non-traditional style. And honestly, don't you wanna
be using olive oil when you're sautéing your vegetables,
you should be sticking with a traditional style. And our favorite here is the one from Pam. If you're somebody who
wants to try something new and you wanna be able to sauté with your cooking spray over high heat, you can use this non-traditional
non-propellant style. The Chosen Foods is our favorite. So there you have it, something simple. Let's keep it simple. - [Narrator] For full episodes of your favorite cooking shows, head over to ATK's full episodes channel on YouTube. You can stream complete seasons of "America's Test Kitchen,"

- I'm talking about one of my favorite
subjects today, mozzarella, and I'm going to explain
the wonderful world of Italian cheese. So let's start with fresh
mozzarella right here. So if it is made with cow's milk, you'll see on the label
sometimes fior di latte. And so this is a high moisture cheese. You really want to use this in a salad with a drizzle of olive
oil, maybe balsamic vinegar. Our favorite brand is Bell Gioioso. Now buffalo mozzarella
is also fresh mozzarella. It's here in the front and
the difference here is made with milk from water buffalo. Now water buffalo herds were
wiped out during World War II, and so the production really switched from water buffalo to cow's milk. But you can still get
this cheese from Italy. The difference is it's got much more fat. It's a little tangier, grassy notes. You wanna make sure that
when you're shopping, you're getting buffalo
mozzarella from Italy, and it's got the DOP sticker on it so that you know you are
getting the real deal. You want to make sure
that you are serving this with some wonderful bread and olive oil. Next up, we've got block mozzarella. Now this is probably a thing
that we're most familiar with and was actually invented
here in the United States by Italian-Americans who wanted a cheese that was a little bit more shelf stable. This is pizza cheese. This is also the cheese that you want to be using in baked
ziti or inside a lasagna. Our favorite brands are
Polly-O and Galbani. Now, for those of you who want shortcuts, and I know you do want your shortcuts, you want the already shredded cheese. It's got anti-caking agents on it, and so you never ever want to eat this in an uncooked application. It is okay in a casserole dish, but if you're gonna buy it, Sargento is the Test Kitchen's favorite. Next up, we have burrata, and this is such a clever invention. It comes from Puglia in the 1950s and somebody had the idea
of taking all those shreds of leftover fresh mozzarella and stuffing them inside
balls of fresh mozzarella and adding cream in the process. Now, I think there are two ways to think about using burrata. You can either put it with
like heirloom tomatoes, it's a little creamier and
sort of milky and runny. I also love it on top of warm dishes. So take a Dutch baby out of the oven, or even a pizza that's
already got block mozzarella and then rip open the
burrata and put it on top. It is so delicious. Our favorite brand is Lioni. Now if you love the center of burrata, it's actually something
called stracciatella. You can use the
stracciatella in the same way that you would use burrata,
except it's even runnier and creamier and more delicious. Next up, we have smoked mozzarella. And as the name indicates,
it is fresh mozzarella that's been smoked. During the smoking process, it loses a fair amount of moisture, so this is a great
choice inside a sandwich where you don't want
the bread to get soggy. It's obviously got a
little bit more personality and you get smoky flavor. And last up, we have scamorza. So this is fresh
mozzarella that is braided during the shaping process and then aged. It's often smoked. It's really lovely in a pasta casserole, also wonderful in sandwiches. So there you have it, all

full pork belly, you'd end up with something closer to this length. On either end of that belly, you're gonna find a good amount of fat, and that's the bit that they trim. Because this ends up being waste, they'll charge you more for this. And so yes, you're getting less fat, but you're also paying for it. Let's talk about nitrite
free and uncured bacon. I have a package of bacon
here that is uncured, and below it it says no
nitrites or nitrates added. So nitrates and nitrites are curing salts. They're used to extend the shelf life. They keep bad bugs from growing
so your bacon will keep. Producers can say that
their bacon is uncured. They didn't use a curing salt or nitrite and nitrate free when they
don't use sodium nitrite or sodium nitrate. That said, there are ingredients out there that contain nitrites,

any nitrites or nitrates. If you check the ingredients
on these packages, you'll often see something
like celery powder or maybe dried celery juice. That's a natural ingredient that contains a good amount of nitrites. And in fact, our reviews team sent a bunch of samples

the nitrite free stuff actually contained more nitrites than something that is cured, where they will disclose that yes, we did use sodium nitrite in this bacon. Last up, we have thick cut bacon. So I have two samples here, and the thick cut bacon is significantly thicker
than the thin cut. This might seem obvious, but the thicker cut bacon takes
a little bit longer to cook. It's got more water in there, and you have to drive that
water off before it gets crispy. So if you really want your
bacon to cook quickly, and if you like crispy bacon, it's really best to stick

you're looking for, the ATK app is a great resource. Bacon's done, I wanna get these strips onto paper towels to drain

## 16. The Best Brand of Breadcrumbs (and Best Ways to Use Them) | America's Test Kitchen
**Channel:** America's Test Kitchen | **Views:** 73K | **Date:** 1 month ago | **Duration:** 3:46 | **ID:** 8vFmUISTAcc
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=8vFmUISTAcc

### Transcript:
- Newsflash from the Test Kitchen: My colleagues are so particular, we will argue about breadcrumbs, yes, breadcrumbs. In fact, we tasted six different brands along with fresh breadcrumbs. And the reason why it matters is because depending on the application, you're going to want a different crumb. Now let me start down here
with fresh bread crumbs. So this is our top rated
sandwich bread by Arnold. You're gonna need a food processor, which I'm not that wild about. You don't need to remove the crusts, grind it maybe 10 pulses. You're gonna get a sort of irregular, fairly coarse, these are very soft, obviously, crumbs. Now there are three ways that we typically use bread crumbs. The first is as a panade inside meatballs or meatloaf. And what the bread crumbs are
doing is they're absorbing the liquid from the milk creating a gel that not only
creates sort of structure so that the meatball stays together, but it helps the meat retain moisture. And so even though that
meatloaf or meatball is cooked until it's well done, it's still tender and juicy. That's the magic of breadcrumbs. These are wonderful as panade. They're not as good when it
comes to a breading around a cutlet because they're so coarse, you get irregular coating
and you get splotchy. And my colleagues don't wanna see any chicken poking through. They want all crust on the outside. They do work well in
the third application, which is on top of a casserole or maybe some macaroni and cheese. Toast these crumbs in a
skillet with some butter, sprinkle 'em over the casserole, and they're more what I
would call crisp crumbs rather than crunchy. Next up, plain breadcrumbs and
plain is really important. No stale herbs here. Now we tested two brands, Progresso and 4C. They were both fine. Progresso, slight favorite. You're gonna notice
here they're very fine. very sandy, in fact. They make a nice coating on a breading because they're very even
so you get nice browning. They don't make a great topping on a casserole or macaroni and cheese. They're just too gritty and sandy, and they're not a very pleasant texture, and they don't absorb
liquid well in a panade. They've got oil in them, and so they just don't do as good a job as the fresh breadcrumbs when it comes to meatloaf or meatballs. Last but definitely not least, because, of course, I do have a favorite and it is the Panko. What I love about them is
they've got a nice large sort of crumb on these. We tested a variety of brands. Kikkoman is our favorite 'cause the crumb is
kind of a medium-sized. Some brands were actually kind
of too crunchy and too large, and some were too fine. Panko's got a really
interesting sort of backstory in how they make it. Its crustless bread, and rather than using
radiant heat and baking it, they put the raw dough on a metal plate and then use electrical
currents to bake the loaf. And you don't have crust. The loaves are then dried and shredded. This makes, I think, the best breading for a cutlet because it's really crispy. It's my favorite on top of a casserole. Same reason. It's super crunchy rather than crispy, like the fresh crumbs. It's okay as a panade. Little pro tip, these crumbs, put 'em inside a zipper lock bag and then whack 'em with a rolling pin and they'll get a little finer and they'll work better as panade. Panko will keep for a very
long time in the pantry, which makes it's super
convenient and always on hand. And if you want it to last forever, just keep it in the freezer. So there you have it. There's a reason to be particular
about your breadcrumbs. - [Speaker] For full episodes of your favorite cooking shows, head over to at ATK's full

some water along the edge to make them easier to seal. I just use my finger for this. And this is just room temperature water. A little goes a long way. Almost like finger painting. And now that the wrappers are prepped, I'm ready to fill them. Because I added soy sauce
in, that's additional salt, so you might get a little
bit of extra liquid that comes out. So you can just push
the filling to the side. And to each wrapper, I'm adding a scant tablespoon of filling. I'm using a mini cookie scoop, but you can also use a regular tablespoon. And to fold these, I'm
keeping it super simple, just a half moon shape. So, I use my fingers to
pack the filling in slightly if you feel like it might leak out. And I like to start from
the center when I fold. That just creates a nice anchoring point, and then pressing firmly around edges, you really wanna get
rid of any air pockets just in case it bursts in the pan. If you feel like the edges
are drying out slightly, you can re-wet it a little bit. Those wet edges are really helping adhere, almost like a glue. And the half moon is the
most simple fold you can do on potstickers, and it's really effective. If you want some more complex pleats, you can also check out
our technique video. And now that the edges are sealed, I'm just going to gently flatten one side. So, pressing it flat with my fingers, this just creates a nice, flat surface for even browning later. So, repeating that with
all of my potstickers. And I'm just going to transfer

continuing on until I reach 24. You can assemble everything now, but you don't actually have
to cook them right away. You can refrigerate
them for up to 24 hours or keep them in the freezer
for up to one month. So, I've made all 24 potstickers and now I'm ready to cook them. I'm just going to keep them covered with my damp towel while

a pan-frying method for cooking our potstickers and that utilizes both

all over a cold skillet. Notice how it applies evenly. If I were to heat up my skillet first, it would begin to pool in

- Restaurant chefs have a trick for when they need to make

in just a few minutes then I would blanch the veg. The goal was to take them to the point where they were just
shy of cooked through. This meant that they were
less likely to be overcooked when it came time to reheat them. Once they were ready, I
transferred them to an ice bath to start the cooking
before packing them up. So why did I bother with all this work? Well, it allowed me to get

them and weigh them again. So clearly they've lost some water weight, cooking damages their
cells, forcing out water, that in turn concentrates flavor. Blanching also breaks down

but they're also snappy. Now, if you're the planning ahead type, then blanching and
chilling your veg is great and if you're not, well that's okay too. You can actually get all of these effects without the cooking. (upbeat music) Blanched vegetables are lightly
seasoned, crisp, tender, and their flavor is intensified, and it turns out you can skip the cooking and the risk of overcooking
and still get these effects with certain vegetables. What you need is salt and pressure. I'm gonna use this green cabbage to demonstrate how this works, but it works with most
veg that you can eat raw. Step one is to prep the veg,
the cabbage in this case. I'm gonna pull off this outer leaf 'cause it's starting to wilt, and we'll just cut this in half. I am gonna chop this into about three quarter inch ish pieces. You don't have to be

salt gets everywhere. Now, kosher salt is coarser and more abrasive than table salt. And that's good because I

## 26. Best Way to Ship Holiday Cookies Across the Country (Packaging Guide) | America's Test Kitchen
**Channel:** America's Test Kitchen | **Views:** 18K | **Date:** 1 month ago | **Duration:** 2:22 | **ID:** eAkCgTYutNE
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=eAkCgTYutNE

### Transcript:
- Giving someone a box of gorgeous homemade
cookies through the mail, it's a wonderful thing, but not if they arrive a shattered mess or if they're stale or mushy. To find out the best way
to ship cookies and bars so they arrive in perfect condition, we made nine different
kinds of cookies and bars, and we boxed them up
in various combinations using different packing materials. We stored the boxes for six
days to simulate shipping time, occasionally tossing 'em around to mimic the indignities
that happen along the way. Then we unpacked them. Lessons learned. First, choose wisely. These cookies did well, ginger snaps, biscotti,
meringues, and spritz cookies. These were all varieties of cookies that are baked until
they're dry throughout. They stayed intact,
crunchy, and fresh-tasting. Moist brownies and pecan
bars also did well. But chewy cookies that started
out just slightly moist, including our perfect
chocolate chip cookies, ended up stale. And delicate lace cookies
arrived as broken shards. Second, remember like with like, both in texture and flavor, moist items like brownies
turned crisp meringues to mush, ginger snaps transferred
their spicy flavors to milder cookies, and not in a good way. Third, you wanna stack bars in small sets. You wanna put parchment between each piece and wrap the whole stack
tightly with plastic wrap. Put cookies in zipper lock bags. Now, how many cookies
we packed into each bag didn't matter as long as
we packed the box properly. So pack with padding. For maximum shock absorption, surround your wrapped cookies with plenty of lightweight bulk, recycled packing peanuts, bubble wrap, or our favorite, air-popped
popcorn, all work well. You wanna put the most delicate items in the very center of the box. And now, you are gonna put 3 to 4 inches of packing material on
all sides, top and bottom. And that's it. Now, your friends and family can enjoy your cookies from far away. - [Julia] For full episodes of your favorite cooking shows, head over to ATK's full
episodes channel on YouTube. You can stream complete seasons of "America's Test Kitchen",
"Cook's Country", and more. See you there.

In terms of making it food safe, you have a couple of options. You can combine the eggs
with booze and sugar, making sure to keep the
alcohol at least at 18%, and then refrigerate
for three weeks minimum to drive off any potential pathogens. That makes something pretty good, but I've got something better and quicker. (egg banging) Our version whisks together
whole eggs plus a couple of extra yolks for richness. You can freeze those whites
for a whiskey sour later, along with sugar and salt. Use it to make a custard with milk. Then bring the whole thing to 160 degrees. This makes it food safe and creates this lovely
lightly thickened texture. The key is gradual heat
and constant stirring, which prevents the eggs at the bottom of the pot from curdling. Strain the nog to catch
any cooked lumps of egg and whisk in a cup of brandy, some vanilla and freshly grated nutmeg. Chill this base completely in the fridge. Now, here's our secret
for incredible texture. Whip heavy cream to soft peaks and then whip it into the chilled base. This gives you a much lighter,
more luxurious texture. It's fluffy and marshmallow like. It's that combo of custard
and a whipped cream. This is a drink that's practically a meal, well, maybe breakfasts. Rich, spiced, completely safe and absolutely delicious. Commercial eggnog doesn't even come close. We loved this version so much

the almond lemon version. So good. It combines amaretto with

non-alcoholic substitution. Use root beer instead of the

Now, what I like to do is to take that overhang and just bend it over the edge like this and just kind of crease it over the edge. That way, it gets out of the way while you're working with the pan. So, now that the pan is ready, we're going to move it off to the side. And the next thing we're going to do is start with our dry ingredients. So, the first ingredient we have is three cups of all-purpose flour. Main traditional shortbread recipes and even modern ones would use things like rice flour, cornstarch, or even oat flour to add different textures and flavors to their shortbread. You can definitely do that, but today we're going to use good old allpurpose flour to keep it really simple. So, the next ingredient we're going to use is 2/3 of a cup or 4 and 2/3 ounces of superfine sugar. Now, superfine sugar is just regular granulated sugar with a smaller grain size. However, if you don't have superfine sugar, that's totally okay. You can also take the same weight of granulated sugar, pop it in a food processor, and pulse it a few times until you get those grains a little smaller, and then use that. So, we're going to add that to our flour next. And then the last dry ingredient we're going to use is half a teaspoon of table salt. And now, I'm just going to whisk together the dry ingredients to make sure they're really evenly mixed in the bowl. So, the next ingredient we're going to talk about is the butter. So, I'm using salted European butter. Traditionally, shortbread is used as a showcase for great dairy. So, salted butter is going to have a lot more flavor than unsalted butter. And also, you want to use the best butter that you can get. And for me, that's European butter. American butter is usually around 80% fat to 20% water. Whereas, European butter can be anywhere between 82 to 85% of fat. So, the reason why you want to use European butter is because it has more fat. More fat equals flavor. And also more fat creates a crumbly, shorter texture. You can absolutely use American butter, but just know that because of that slightly higher water content, the cookies will be slightly chewier as well, but that's totally okay. So, this is 2 and 1/2 sticks or 20 tablespoons of salted European butter. And you want to make sure that your butter is softened to room temperature, so it's much easier to incorporate into the dry ingredients. Next, next ingredient for the shortbread is vanilla beans. So, this recipe uses two vanilla beans, but we're not going to use the whole bean. And we're actually just going to take the seeds from inside the bean out. So, I'm going to show you how I do that. Put your finger on the kind of hooked end on one side right here to kind of hold it down to the board. Take the tip of your pairing knife, insert right at the top of the bean in the middle, and then you just want to cut straight down the middle to kind of split it into two halves that are still connected by that little piece right there. So, the next thing you're going to do is hold that piece that you had your finger on. Turn the vanilla bean so that the halves are facing upwards. And then you want to take the back of your pairing knife, the less sharp side, press it up against the vanilla bean, push away from you to kind of scrape all those vanilla bean seeds out, and give it a few more scraping just to make sure you get all those seeds out from the vanilla bean pod. And then we're going to turn it over and get the other half just like we did the first half. So, I like to use vanilla beans in this recipe as well because you can actually see the vanilla beans in the cookie. You can see the speckles of those vanilla bean seeds and that's really nice. Now, you want to save your vanilla bean pods because these have a lot more flavor in them. So, you can add them to sugar to make vanilla sugar. You can add them to custards or cream to make ice cream. They have a lot of good life left in them. So, definitely save those. So, now I'm going to add the vanilla bean seeds directly to the butter and the dry ingredients. So now that we have our butter and vanilla beans in the dry ingredients, you could stop here if you wanted to. This would be a classic shortbread. It's got your dry ingredients, your sugar, your butter, and a little bit of flavor. But we want to take these in a slightly more elevated direction. Again, these are salted vanilla bean shortbread. So we want to emphasize that saltiness using salted European butter. Adding a little more salt than you normally would to these cookies. So to play off that, I like to add some chopped fresh rosemary. It kind of has this woodsy aroma that makes the cookies really nice for a holiday cookie platter or for a cheese board any other time of the year. And if you don't want to use rosemary, you can definitely use thyme. Here, we're going to use 1 and 1/2 teaspoon of fresh minced rosemary. So, now that we have all the ingredients in the bowl, it's time to get mixing. And the great thing about this, you don't need a whisk or rubber spatula or anything. You just need your hands. So, just like making a pie dough or biscuits, you want to rub the dry ingredients into the butter until you get this nice, really crumbly dough that's uniform all over in texture. And that ensures that the salt and the rosemary and the vanilla bean are really well and evenly incorporated in the dough. So, while I'm mixing the dough here, let me talk to you about the 3:1 ratio of shortbread. You know, most classic cookies don't follow a specific formula, but shortbread does, and that's why it's been so easy and so popular over the centuries. And so it's three parts of flour to two parts of fat and one part sugar. And that's all by weight. And the great thing about that is is that you don't really need a recipe for making shortbread. As long as you know that ratio, you can make your own recipe. And then you can flavor it however you want to. So now that the dough is ready to go, this is what you want to look for right here. You want evenly sized crumbles of butter and flour. All the rosemary and vanilla bean seeds should be really well and evenly mixed. Should feel kind of like clumpy sand. Shouldn't be too sticky or nor too dry. It should be just perfect like this. Now, it's time to transfer the dough to the baking pan. I want you to resist the urge to just dump all the dough into the pan, then start spreading because what that does is it can like pull the dough like when you're rolling out a pie crust or something similar to that, and it makes the dough more tough and less tender. And what we're going for is tender here. So, what I like to do, especially with a crumbly dough like this, which makes it so easy, is just pick up handfuls of the dough and just sprinkle it evenly over the bottom of the pan. We're not pressing or pushing or pulling anything yet. We just want to transfer the dough and just evenly kind of sprinkle it. Let it fall out of your fingers into all the corners. And now once you have all your crumbles in the pan, nice and loose just like this. Use your fingertips to just spread everything evenly kind of like you're raking sand or rocks in. Make sure to get the corners. Make sure you don't have any kind of bulging middles. Then once you are sure you have everything exactly where you want it to be, now we're going to start pressing. So you just want to use your hands to just very lightly press the dough down. Make sure to get your fingertips into the corners. Don't have to press that hard. Just gonna lightly compact it. So, now that all the dough is lightly compacted in the pan like this, you can definitely stop here if you wanted to, but we want to make these really nice and clean and professional looking. So, what I like to do is use a dry measuring cup and take the bottom of it, which is nice and flat, and use that to press the top of the dough so you have a nice flat even surface of cookie dough. So, you just want to gently press all over the dough. And as you can see as I go around, it just makes it a little bit smoother. This will also make the cookie dough easier to cut later because it'll be a little more compact and not so crumbly. So, now that the dough has been flattened out on top, we're going to dock it with a fork about 20 times. Now, there's two reasons to do this. One is really practical. When you dock a pie crust for blind baking or scoring the top of, let's say, a sourdough loaf of bread, it helps to release steam from inside whatever you're baking so that it makes the crumb a little lighter and less dense. So, the second reason involves a little more folklore. It used to be bad luck to cut the shortbread after it was baked. So, you wanted to dock it or score it to make some fault lines so that after it was baked, you could then snap it by hand. So, however you get around to docking the top of your dough, one thing we can all agree on is it looks really pretty and it's also very iconic. But one way that we're going to kind of change up the top of the shortbread here is by adding again a little more salt. Again, this is salted vanilla bean shortbread. So, we want to add some crunchy, flaky sea salt to the top of this shortbread just to add a little more crunch and kind of enhance that savoriness that we're going with with the extra salt, the European butter, and the rosemary. We're using about one teaspoon of flaky sea salt. So, now that all the flaky sea salt is on top of the shortbread, tamp down with your hands just to make sure that salt is sticking into the dough. So, now the shortbread is ready to bake. So, I'm going to bake the shortbread in a 300°ree oven with the rack in the middle position for about 60 to 70 minutes until it's light golden brown and just dry to the touch, remembering to rotate the pan halfway through baking. So, the shortbread have just come out of the oven. We're going to place them on a rack to cool. As you can see, they're just starting to brown on top. The edges should just be very light golden brown, and also it should be dry to the touch on top. So, while the shortbread is still hot, this is the time to cut it. If you wait to cut the shortbread until after it's cooled, it can kind of break apart into irregular pieces, crumbly chunks. We don't want that. We want nice clean sides. So, what I like to do is first cut three lengthwise columns. So, I just kind of eyeball it. Just cut into the dough. You can use a pairing knife. You can use a bench scraper, whatever you find easiest. And you'll see that because the dough is still hot, it's really soft and easy to cut. And now, I like to turn the pan crosswise like this. And we want 12 crosswise rows. Now to get that, what I like to do first is cut the dough in half. So that way I know where my halfway point is. And so now we know that each half will be six rows. So we have 12 total. So I want to then cut each half right here into quarters. So now we have four sections. 12 divided 4 is three. So we're going to take each of these four sections we have here and cut them into three rows. These you can just kind of eyeball. If you want to use a ruler, be really professional about it. You absolutely can. But the kind of nice thing about shortbread is that they are rustic, so you don't have to be too precise about it. So now that we have all the shortbread cut, we just need to let them cool completely. So now that the shortbread have cooled completely, it's time to take them out of the pan and taste them. So remember those overhanging pieces of parchment paper that were on the pan before? We're going to grab those and those are going to help us get the shortbread out of the pan now. So now we have our perfectly cut and cooled shortbread and let's try one. So now I'm going to go for corner piece. Those are my favorites. It's really cool to see all the vanilla beans that are in the crumb right there. You can have the flaky sea salt on top. Nice snappy texture. Nice kind of crumbly and toasty on the inside. They're perfectly sweet. You can definitely taste the saltiness. The rosemary really helps to kind of bridge that gap between saltiness, sweetness. They're really crumbly and crunchy. It's a perfect cookie. When it comes to simple but memorable cookie recipes, shortbread is an excellent one to have in your back [music] pocket. By adding vanilla, fragrant rosemary, and flaky sea salt, we turn this version into a real conversation starter. It's a little sweet, a little savory, and completely delicious. Now that you've completed this lesson, you're ready to try other things. And with ATK classes, [music] you'll get access to a wide variety of lessons filled with masterful techniques, helpful videos, and step-by-step photos. From knife skills and making handrolled pasta to baking [music] and frosting incredible cakes, and so much more. Download the America's Test Kitchen app and start learning with ATK classes today.
